        "url": "https://moroccobeautyspots.com/blog/getting-around-morocco",
        "title": "Getting Around Morocco: Every Transport Option | MBS",
        "description": "# Getting Around Morocco: An Honest Map of Every Option\nUpdated August 21, 202610 min readByYoussef El Alaoui\n\n![Getting Around Morocco: An Honest Map of Every Option](https://moroccobeautyspots.com/_next/image?url=%2Fblog%2Fgetting-around-morocco%2Fhero.jpg&w=1920&q=65)\n\nAn operator's honest map of getting around Morocco: when the train wins, when you need a grand taxi, bus, rental car, or a private driver — with a clear cost comparison.\n\n**On the Tangier–Casablanca–Marrakech spine, the train is the best way to get around Morocco — fast, cheap, comfortable.** **Off that spine, you choose between a grand taxi, a CTM or Supratours bus, a rental car, or a private driver.** Everything below is how to pick.\n\nI move guests around this country for a living, so I see what actually works and what quietly ruins a day. The honest truth is that Morocco gives you a genuinely excellent option for one half of the map and a patchwork of trade-offs for the other half. Nobody tells you that cleanly, so here it is. This is the pillar I point people to before we ever talk routes — the full menu, with the catches stated plainly, so you can read your own trip onto it.\n\nThink of the country in two layers. There is the populated Atlantic belt and the imperial cities, stitched together by rail and motorway, where independent travel is easy and cheap. Then there is everything that made you want to come — the Sahara, the High Atlas passes, the blue lanes of the Rif, the small coastal towns — none of which a train will ever reach. Match the right tool to each layer and the whole trip gets simpler.\n\n## When do CTM and Supratours buses make sense?\nIntercity coaches win when you want budget travel to a town the train doesn't reach and you don't mind a fixed timetable. The two names that matter are CTM and Supratours — both reputable, air-conditioned, and bookable online or at the terminal.\n\nCTM (Compagnie de Transports au Maroc) is the country's oldest line and has the widest national network, reaching small towns in the north and south that nothing else serves. Supratours is ONCF's own coach arm, and it exists specifically to plug the gaps the rail map leaves — the classic examples are Marrakech to Essaouira and Marrakech to Agadir, where the Supratours coach effectively continues your train journey to the coast. Both are safe, comfortable, and cheap. The catch is rigidity: you go when the timetable says, you stop where it stops, and on a scenic route like the road over the Atlas a coach blows past every viewpoint without slowing. For a single A-to-B hop to a beach town, a coach is the right, frugal call. For a journey where the landscape is the point, it's the wrong tool.\n\n## How do petit taxis work inside the cities?\nPetit taxis are the small, metered, colour-coded cabs that handle short trips inside a single city — red in Casablanca, ochre in Marrakech, blue in Rabat. They take a maximum of three passengers and, by law, can't leave the city limits.\n\nFor getting across town — station to neighbourhood, hotel to restaurant — the petit taxi is the everyday tool, and it's inexpensive. The one rule that saves you money and friction: insist on the meter (\"compteur,\" please) at the start of the ride. Most drivers use it without prompting; a few will quote a flat tourist price instead, which is usually higher. Because petit taxis are capped at three passengers and can't cross municipal boundaries, two things trip people up. A family of four needs two cabs or a grand taxi. And no petit taxi can take you from one city to the airport of another, or out to a site beyond the city edge — that's grand-taxi or private-transfer territory. Inside the walls, though, they're the quick, cheap default.\n\n## When is a private driver actually worth it?\nIt's not worth paying for on the easy city-to-city legs the train already does well.\n\nI'll be straight about this, because we sell driver-led trips and I still don't think you need one everywhere. On the northern spine, the train is better and cheaper — take it. Where a driver genuinely changes the trip is the route that made you book Morocco in the first place: the Tizi n'Tichka pass, the Road of a Thousand Kasbahs, the long run out to [Merzouga or Zagora for the Sahara](https://moroccobeautyspots.com/blog/sahara-merzouga-vs-zagora). Those are four-to-nine-hour mountain-and-desert days with a dozen stops worth making, car-free towns a taxi can't reach, and roads you'd rather watch than wrestle. As guests put it afterwards, you get far more than transport. The cleanest version of all this is the [Marrakech-to-Fes desert route](https://moroccobeautyspots.com/blog/how-to-get-from-marrakech-to-fes), where a driver turns a tedious transfer into the best days of the trip.\n\n## Do Uber and Careem work in Morocco?\nNot reliably — don't build a plan around either. Careem, the Dubai-based app, has only ever operated intermittently and in a legal grey zone, mostly in Casablanca and Rabat. In practice, ride-hailing is not something a tourist can count on, so treat it as a maybe at best, never your default.\n\nThe real in-city option is the metered petit taxi. Hail one on the street, insist on the meter (\"compteur\"), or agree the fare before you set off, and it does exactly what a ride app would, for less.\n\n## Are domestic flights worth it in Morocco?\nOnly for one or two specific long hops — chiefly to or from the far south, like Dakhla or Laayoune, where driving would eat a day or more. For the classic northern circuit, flying usually costs you more time than it saves once you count airport faff.\n\nRoyal Air Maroc and budget carriers like Air Arabia Maroc link the main airports, and on paper a 75-minute flight beats a seven-hour drive. In practice, the check-in, security, and the transfers to and from two airports often erase the saving on anything but the longest routes — Casablanca or Marrakech down to the deep south being the obvious exception. For everything inside the imperial-cities-and-desert core, you'll move more smoothly, and see more, by combining the train for the city legs with a car for the landscapes.\n\n## How should I handle airport transfers?\nFor your very first ride and your last, book a private transfer in advance — it's the cheapest insurance on the trip. Arriving jet-lagged into a new-country airport and negotiating a fare in a language you don't speak is where good trips get a sour first hour.\n\nThe friction is real and specific. At Marrakech's Menara airport, the medina is only about six kilometres away, but no taxi can drive to a riad's door inside the car-free old town — so you're often dropped at a gate to find the rest on foot with luggage, after haggling. A pre-arranged driver who meets you by name, has a fixed price, and walks you to the riad removes the whole problem at the moment you're least equipped for it. After that first ride, the city's petit taxis are fine. We cover why that first transfer matters in our guide to [Marrakech airport private transfers](https://moroccobeautyspots.com/blog/marrakech-airport-private-transfer). It's the one piece of ground transport I'd never wing on arrival day.\n\n| Mode | Best for | Rough cost | The honest caveat |\n|-|-|-|-|\n| Train (Al Boraq / Al Atlas / TNR) | Al Boraq: Tangier–Kenitra–Rabat Agdal–Casa Voyageurs (does not serve Marrakech or Fes); regular trains serve Marrakech and Fes | Check live ONCF fare | Al Boraq does not serve Marrakech, Fes, or the airport; no trains reach the desert or Atlas |\n| CTM / Supratours bus | Budget hops to towns off the rail line (e.g. Essaouira, Agadir) | Low — often under ~150 MAD | Fixed timetable; blows past every scenic stop |\n| Grand taxi (shared) | Short links between nearby towns, no station | A few MAD per seat; more to buy out the car | Six to a car, no meter, assertive driving; confirm price first |\n| Petit taxi (in-city) | Short trips inside one city | Usually well under ~50 MAD metered | Max 3 passengers; can't leave city limits; insist on the meter |\n| Uber / Careem | Not a reliable option for tourists | n/a | Uber left in 2018; Careem intermittent/grey-zone — use a metered petit taxi instead |\n| Domestic flight | Far-south long hauls (Dakhla, Laayoune) | Varies by carrier/season | Airport time erases the saving on shorter routes |\n| Private airport transfer | Arrival and departure day | Fixed quoted fare | Worth booking ahead; not needed for routine city trips |\n\n## In summary\nGetting around Morocco isn't one decision, it's a series of small ones, and the map makes most of them for you. Take Al Boraq on its Tangier–Kenitra–Rabat Agdal–Casa Voyageurs corridor and regular ONCF mainline trains for Marrakech and Fes. Inside a city, use metered petit taxis. For short links to off-rail towns, a grand taxi or a CTM/Supratours coach does the job on a budget. Match each leg to the right tool and Morocco gets dramatically easier to travel.\n\nIf you'd rather not assemble all of that yourself, that's the whole case for handing the moving parts to someone who does it daily — not because you couldn't, but because the desert-and-Atlas legs are genuinely where a local driver-guide earns it, while you keep the train for the easy city hops. It's one option with real trade-offs, not the only way to see Morocco. When you're ready to map your own version, tell us your dates and who's travelling and we'll sequence the hybrid route for you with our [trip planner](https://moroccobeautyspots.com/planner).\n\n![Youssef El Alaoui](https://moroccobeautyspots.com/_next/image?url=%2Fteam%2Fyoussef.jpg&w=1920&q=75)\n\nWritten by\n\nYoussef El Alaoui\n\nLead Morocco Specialist\n\nBorn in Fes, based in Marrakech.\n\nContinue exploring",
        "position": 7,
        "markdown": "- [Home](https://moroccobeautyspots.com/)\n- [Destinations](https://moroccobeautyspots.com/destinations)\n- [Tours](https://moroccobeautyspots.com/tours)\n- [Planner](https://moroccobeautyspots.com/planner)\n- [Blog](https://moroccobeautyspots.com/blog)\n- [Contact](https://moroccobeautyspots.com/contact)\n\n🇬🇧🇫🇷🇪🇸🇩🇪🇵🇹🇷🇺\n\nPractical\n\n# Getting Around Morocco: An Honest Map of Every Option\n\nUpdated August 21, 202610 min readByYoussef El Alaoui\n\n![Getting Around Morocco: An Honest Map of Every Option](https://moroccobeautyspots.com/_next/image?url=%2Fblog%2Fgetting-around-morocco%2Fhero.jpg&w=1920&q=65)\n\nAn operator's honest map of getting around Morocco: when the train wins, when you need a grand taxi, bus, rental car, or a private driver — with a clear cost comparison.\n\n**On the Tangier–Casablanca–Marrakech spine, the train is the best way to get around Morocco — fast, cheap, comfortable. Off that spine, you choose between a grand taxi, a CTM or Supratours bus, a rental car, or a private driver.** Everything below is how to pick.\n\nI move guests around this country for a living, so I see what actually works and what quietly ruins a day. The honest truth is that Morocco gives you a genuinely excellent option for one half of the map and a patchwork of trade-offs for the other half. Nobody tells you that cleanly, so here it is. This is the pillar I point people to before we ever talk routes — the full menu, with the catches stated plainly, so you can read your own trip onto it.\n\nThink of the country in two layers. There is the populated Atlantic belt and the imperial cities, stitched together by rail and motorway, where independent travel is easy and cheap. Then there is everything that made you want to come — the Sahara, the High Atlas passes, the blue lanes of the Rif, the small coastal towns — none of which a train will ever reach. Match the right tool to each layer and the whole trip gets simpler.\n\n## What is the fastest, easiest way to get around Morocco?\n\nFor the big cities on the rail line — Tangier, Rabat, Casablanca, Marrakech, Fes — the train, run by the national operator ONCF, beats every other option on time, cost, and comfort. Off the line, it's road transport, and the choice is about how much control you want.\n\nHere's the mental model I give first-timers. If both ends of a journey are cities on the train map, take the train and don't overthink it — the motorway parallels the line, so you skip the traffic and arrive in the city centre. The moment one end is a desert, a mountain pass, or a small town, the rails stop being an option and you're choosing between a shared grand taxi, an intercity coach, a rental car, or a private driver. Almost every Morocco trip is a hybrid of those two worlds, and the people who travel well are the ones who stop trying to force a single mode to do everything. The desert leg and the city leg are different problems with different right answers.\n\n## How good are Morocco's trains, really?\n\nGenuinely excellent, and the best rail network in Africa. The headline is Al Boraq, the continent's first high-speed train — in service since November 2018, running up to 320 km/h between Tangier and Casablanca. The rest of the network is modern intercity service, mostly branded \"Al Atlas.\"\n\nAl Boraq cut the Tangier–Casablanca run from a grinding five hours to about 2h10. Its published stations are Tangier, Kenitra, Rabat Agdal, and Casa Voyageurs; frequency varies, so check the current ONCF timetable. South and east of Casablanca you ride regular Al Atlas/mainline trains to Marrakech and Fes — those legs are not Al Boraq or high-speed. Check live ONCF times and fares rather than relying on a static figure. I tell people to ride Al Boraq once just for the experience; the full picture is in our guide to [Morocco by train and riding Al Boraq](https://moroccobeautyspots.com/blog/morocco-by-train-al-boraq).\n\n## When do CTM and Supratours buses make sense?\n\nIntercity coaches win when you want budget travel to a town the train doesn't reach and you don't mind a fixed timetable. The two names that matter are CTM and Supratours — both reputable, air-conditioned, and bookable online or at the terminal.\n\nCTM (Compagnie de Transports au Maroc) is the country's oldest line and has the widest national network, reaching small towns in the north and south that nothing else serves. Supratours is ONCF's own coach arm, and it exists specifically to plug the gaps the rail map leaves — the classic examples are Marrakech to Essaouira and Marrakech to Agadir, where the Supratours coach effectively continues your train journey to the coast. Both are safe, comfortable, and cheap. The catch is rigidity: you go when the timetable says, you stop where it stops, and on a scenic route like the road over the Atlas a coach blows past every viewpoint without slowing. For a single A-to-B hop to a beach town, a coach is the right, frugal call. For a journey where the landscape is the point, it's the wrong tool.\n\n## What is a grand taxi, and should I use one?\n\nA grand taxi is a shared, fixed-route car — usually an ageing Mercedes or Peugeot — that seats six paying passengers and leaves when it's full. It's the workhorse that connects towns with no train station, and it's cheap, frequent, and very local.\n\nHere's how it actually works. Grand taxis wait at a designated rank, normally near the main bus terminal or a city gate, and each route has a roughly standardised per-seat fare. You pay for one seat and squeeze in — two passengers up front beside the driver, four across the back — or you can \"buy out\" the empty seats to leave immediately and travel in comfort, which is what many of our guests do for short hops. Fares aren't metered, so confirm the per-seat price before you get in; mild haggling is normal for visitors. Grand taxis are perfect for a 30-to-90-minute link between nearby towns. For a long cross-country leg they're cramped, the driving can be assertive, and there are no seatbelt guarantees — which is exactly why most travellers step up to a bus, a rental, or a driver once the distance grows.\n\n## How do petit taxis work inside the cities?\n\nPetit taxis are the small, metered, colour-coded cabs that handle short trips inside a single city — red in Casablanca, ochre in Marrakech, blue in Rabat. They take a maximum of three passengers and, by law, can't leave the city limits.\n\nFor getting across town — station to neighbourhood, hotel to restaurant — the petit taxi is the everyday tool, and it's inexpensive. The one rule that saves you money and friction: insist on the meter (\"compteur,\" please) at the start of the ride. Most drivers use it without prompting; a few will quote a flat tourist price instead, which is usually higher. Because petit taxis are capped at three passengers and can't cross municipal boundaries, two things trip people up. A family of four needs two cabs or a grand taxi. And no petit taxi can take you from one city to the airport of another, or out to a site beyond the city edge — that's grand-taxi or private-transfer territory. Inside the walls, though, they're the quick, cheap default.\n\n## Should I rent a car in Morocco?\n\nRent a car if you're a confident driver who wants total independence and you're sticking mostly to open roads and the coast. It's freeing and affordable. It's the wrong call if your route runs through high mountain passes, car-free medinas, or long unlit rural stretches at night.\n\nThe roads themselves are largely good — the main passes are paved and the motorways modern. What catches people is everything around the driving: hairpin bends with real drop-offs, livestock and pedestrians on rural roads, frequent police checkpoints with sudden speed-limit drops, dark and often unmarked roads after sunset, and the simple fact that your car cannot enter Fes el-Bali or the Marrakech medina — it stops at the gate and you walk or hire a porter. The hidden costs — fuel, motorway tolls, and paid parking on the edge of every medina — also add up beyond the day rate. We lay out the road conditions in full in [whether it's safe to drive in Morocco](https://moroccobeautyspots.com/blog/is-it-safe-to-drive-in-morocco), and the true all-in cost against a driver in [renting a car versus hiring a driver](https://moroccobeautyspots.com/blog/rent-a-car-vs-driver-morocco). For a relaxed coastal loop, a rental is great. For the Atlas-and-desert grand tour, most of our guests are happier not behind the wheel.\n\n## When is a private driver actually worth it?\n\nA private driver-guide earns its place when the drive itself is the experience, when you're crossing terrain that's hard or stressful to self-drive, or when you simply don't want to manage logistics. It's not worth paying for on the easy city-to-city legs the train already does well.\n\nI'll be straight about this, because we sell driver-led trips and I still don't think you need one everywhere. On the northern spine, the train is better and cheaper — take it. Where a driver genuinely changes the trip is the route that made you book Morocco in the first place: the Tizi n'Tichka pass, the Road of a Thousand Kasbahs, the long run out to [Merzouga or Zagora for the Sahara](https://moroccobeautyspots.com/blog/sahara-merzouga-vs-zagora). Those are four-to-nine-hour mountain-and-desert days with a dozen stops worth making, car-free towns a taxi can't reach, and roads you'd rather watch than wrestle. The drivers we work with read the checkpoints, take the cliff edge so you watch the view, know which viewpoint is worth stopping for, and get you to the dunes fresh instead of wrung out. As guests put it afterwards, you get far more than transport. The cleanest version of all this is the [Marrakech-to-Fes desert route](https://moroccobeautyspots.com/blog/how-to-get-from-marrakech-to-fes), where a driver turns a tedious transfer into the best days of the trip.\n\n## Do Uber and Careem work in Morocco?\n\nNot reliably — don't build a plan around either. Uber pulled out of Morocco in 2018 and has not returned as of 2026. Careem, the Dubai-based app, has only ever operated intermittently and in a legal grey zone, mostly in Casablanca and Rabat. In practice, ride-hailing is not something a tourist can count on, so treat it as a maybe at best, never your default.\n\nWhat this means on the ground: there is no dependable ride-hailing app you can open the way you would at home, and there is none at all once you leave the big cities — nothing in Chefchaouen, Merzouga, or the medinas. The real in-city option is the metered petit taxi. Hail one on the street, insist on the meter (\"compteur\"), or agree the fare before you set off, and it does exactly what a ride app would, for less. We keep two honest, regularly updated breakdowns for the detail: [whether Uber works in Morocco](https://moroccobeautyspots.com/blog/is-there-uber-in-morocco) and [whether Careem works in Morocco](https://moroccobeautyspots.com/blog/is-there-careem-in-morocco). For intercity travel, ride apps are simply the wrong tool — that's trains, buses, and drivers.\n\n## Are domestic flights worth it in Morocco?\n\nOnly for one or two specific long hops — chiefly to or from the far south, like Dakhla or Laayoune, where driving would eat a day or more. For the classic northern circuit, flying usually costs you more time than it saves once you count airport faff.\n\nRoyal Air Maroc and budget carriers like Air Arabia Maroc link the main airports, and on paper a 75-minute flight beats a seven-hour drive. In practice, the check-in, security, and the transfers to and from two airports often erase the saving on anything but the longest routes — Casablanca or Marrakech down to the deep south being the obvious exception. For everything inside the imperial-cities-and-desert core, you'll move more smoothly, and see more, by combining the train for the city legs with a car for the landscapes. I almost never route guests onto a domestic flight unless they're tacking on the Atlantic far south or are genuinely short on days and willing to trade scenery for speed.\n\n## How should I handle airport transfers?\n\nFor your very first ride and your last, book a private transfer in advance — it's the cheapest insurance on the trip. Arriving jet-lagged into a new-country airport and negotiating a fare in a language you don't speak is where good trips get a sour first hour.\n\nThe friction is real and specific. At Marrakech's Menara airport, the medina is only about six kilometres away, but no taxi can drive to a riad's door inside the car-free old town — so you're often dropped at a gate to find the rest on foot with luggage, after haggling. A pre-arranged driver who meets you by name, has a fixed price, and walks you to the riad removes the whole problem at the moment you're least equipped for it. After that first ride, the city's petit taxis are fine. We cover why that first transfer matters in our guide to [Marrakech airport private transfers](https://moroccobeautyspots.com/blog/marrakech-airport-private-transfer). It's the one piece of ground transport I'd never wing on arrival day.\n\n| Mode | Best for | Rough cost | The honest caveat |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| Train (Al Boraq / Al Atlas / TNR) | Al Boraq: Tangier–Kenitra–Rabat Agdal–Casa Voyageurs (does not serve Marrakech or Fes); regular trains serve Marrakech and Fes | Check live ONCF fare | Al Boraq does not serve Marrakech, Fes, or the airport; no trains reach the desert or Atlas |\n| CTM / Supratours bus | Budget hops to towns off the rail line (e.g. Essaouira, Agadir) | Low — often under ~150 MAD | Fixed timetable; blows past every scenic stop |\n| Grand taxi (shared) | Short links between nearby towns, no station | A few MAD per seat; more to buy out the car | Six to a car, no meter, assertive driving; confirm price first |\n| Petit taxi (in-city) | Short trips inside one city | Usually well under ~50 MAD metered | Max 3 passengers; can't leave city limits; insist on the meter |\n| Rental car | Confident drivers, coastal loops, full independence | ~Mid range/day plus fuel, tolls, parking | Hard passes, night roads, car-free medinas; one-way drops cost a lot |\n| Private driver + car | Atlas, Sahara, multi-region loops, families | Premium — but close to a rental's true all-in | Overkill on the easy train legs you could ride cheaply |\n| Uber / Careem | Not a reliable option for tourists | n/a | Uber left in 2018; Careem intermittent/grey-zone — use a metered petit taxi instead |\n| Domestic flight | Far-south long hauls (Dakhla, Laayoune) | Varies by carrier/season | Airport time erases the saving on shorter routes |\n| Private airport transfer | Arrival and departure day | Fixed quoted fare | Worth booking ahead; not needed for routine city trips |\n\nGetting around Morocco at a glance — always confirm live fares with the operator.\n\nGetting around Morocco at a glance — always confirm live fares with the operator.\n\n## In summary\n\nGetting around Morocco isn't one decision, it's a series of small ones, and the map makes most of them for you. Take Al Boraq on its Tangier–Kenitra–Rabat Agdal–Casa Voyageurs corridor and regular ONCF mainline trains for Marrakech and Fes. Inside a city, use metered petit taxis. For short links to off-rail towns, a grand taxi or a CTM/Supratours coach does the job on a budget. And for the parts that made you fall for this country — the Sahara, the Atlas passes, the Road of a Thousand Kasbahs, the small coastal and mountain towns — a private driver stops being a luxury and starts being the thing that turns transit into the trip. Match each leg to the right tool and Morocco gets dramatically easier to travel.\n\nIf you'd rather not assemble all of that yourself, that's the whole case for handing the moving parts to someone who does it daily — not because you couldn't, but because the desert-and-Atlas legs are genuinely where a local driver-guide earns it, while you keep the train for the easy city hops. It's one option with real trade-offs, not the only way to see Morocco. When you're ready to map your own version, tell us your dates and who's travelling and we'll sequence the hybrid route for you with our [trip planner](https://moroccobeautyspots.com/planner).\n\n![Youssef El Alaoui](https://moroccobeautyspots.com/_next/image?url=%2Fteam%2Fyoussef.jpg&w=1920&q=75)\n\nWritten by\n\nYoussef El Alaoui\n\nLead Morocco Specialist\n\nBorn in Fes, based in Marrakech.         "url": "https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/morocco-train-travel-guide",
        "title": "Morocco Train Travel Guide 2026: ONCF Routes, Tickets & Tips",
        "description": "# Morocco Train Travel: The Complete Guide to ONCF Rail (2026)\nOctober 23, 2026\n\n8 min read\n\nHow to travel Morocco by train: routes, tickets and tips for the Al Boraq high-speed service and Al Atlas express network — the most comfortable way to connect the imperial cities.\n\n1,549 words\n\n8 min read\n\nWritten and checked by the [Serenity Morocco Tours travel desk](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/about/team) — Travel designers, drivers and licensed guides\n\nLast reviewedOctober 23, 2026\n\nNewsletter\n\nGet Morocco Travel Insights\n\nSubscribe\n\nMorocco's rail network is one of Africa's most developed — clean, reasonably punctual and a genuine pleasure for journeys that matter: Casablanca to Rabat in under an hour, Rabat to Fes in around three hours, Casablanca to Marrakech in roughly three. Operated by ONCF (Office National des Chemins de Fer du Maroc), the network spans roughly 2,000 km and links Morocco's largest cities with a mix of high-speed and classic express services. The flagship Al Boraq TGV-style train slashed the journey from Tangier to Casablanca to around two hours; the Al Atlas expresses handle the inland routes. For travellers doing a multi-city Morocco itinerary — imperial cities, Atlantic coast, Sahara gateway towns — knowing how to use the trains well means faster connections, a lower environmental footprint and a more relaxed arrival than a long road transfer. This guide covers every main route, how to book, what to expect on board and when a private transfer still makes more sense.\n\n## [#](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/morocco-train-travel-guide#at-a-glance) At a Glance\n| | | |---|---| | **Operator** | ONCF (Office National des Chemins de Fer) | | **High-speed service** | Al Boraq: Tangier ↔ Casablanca, roughly 2h10m | | **Classic express** | Al Atlas: Casablanca ↔ Marrakech, Casablanca ↔ Fes, Fes ↔ Oujda | | **Booking** | oncf.ma website, ONCF app, or station counters | | **Book ahead** | Up to around 3 months in advance; earlier is usually cheaper | | **High-speed to Marrakech** | Under construction; tentative completion around 2029 | | **Airport connection** | Direct train, Casablanca airport ↔ Casablanca Voyageurs, roughly 30 minutes |\n\n## [#](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/morocco-train-travel-guide#the-routes-that-still-require-road-travel) The Routes That Still Require Road Travel\nA few popular Morocco itinerary legs are not yet served by rail, and a private transfer or bus is the practical option:\n\n- **Marrakech to Essaouira:** no rail link — roughly 2h30m–3h by private car\n- **Marrakech to Agadir:** no rail link — around 3–4 hours by road\n- **Fes to Chefchaouen:** no rail — best by private car or CTM bus (around 2h30m–3h)\n- **Marrakech to the Sahara (Merzouga or Zagora):** no train to the desert; private car over one or two days is the standard choice\n\nUntil then, the Al Atlas Casablanca–Marrakech service is the only rail option for the Red City. For road-transfer logistics, our [Marrakech to Fes travel guide](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/marrakech-to-fes-travel-guide) weighs all the options, and [private driver versus self-drive](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/morocco-private-driver-vs-self-drive) is useful reading before you decide.\n\n## [#](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/morocco-train-travel-guide#booking-and-practical-tips) Booking and Practical Tips\n**Where to book:** Tickets are available on the **oncf.ma** website, the ONCF mobile app, or at any station counter. Tickets typically go on sale around three months ahead; early purchase gives you lower fares and more seat choice.\n\n**At the station:**\n\n- Arrive 15–20 minutes early at busy hubs like Casablanca Voyageurs or Marrakech\n- Some stations have ticket barriers that require composting (stamping) your ticket before the platform — look for the small yellow machines near the gates\n\n**Class choice:** Second class is comfortable and fine for most journeys. First class is noticeably quieter on the longer Al Atlas runs and worth the modest premium on a 3-hour journey.\n\n**Children and discounts:** ONCF typically offers reduced fares for young children and occasional promotional passes (such as the SAYFI summer pass); confirm current terms and validity directly on oncf.ma as promotions change frequently.\n\nFor travellers who prefer a seamless arrival — a private transfer from the station, a guide ready at the riad — our [Marrakech tours](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/marrakech-tours), [Fes tours](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/fes-tours) and [Casablanca tours](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/casablanca-tours) teams handle the logistics end-to-end. The [imperial cities circuit](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/imperial-cities) is a natural fit for combining the rail network with private guiding.\n\n## [#](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/morocco-train-travel-guide#faq) FAQ\n**Is train travel in Morocco comfortable?** Broadly yes, for city-to-city journeys. The Al Boraq high-speed trains are modern and well-maintained. Al Atlas expresses are older but clean, air-conditioned and reliable.\n\n**How much does a Morocco train ticket cost?** Prices vary by route, service and booking date. Al Boraq second-class tickets have started from around 99–150 MAD for Tangier–Casablanca; Al Atlas second-class fares for Casablanca–Marrakech or Casablanca–Fes are typically lower. Check oncf.ma for current fares — early booking is cheaper.\n\n**Do I need to book Morocco train tickets in advance?** It's advisable, especially in July and August, at Eid holidays and on Al Boraq. Tickets go on sale around three months ahead on oncf.ma and at station counters. Same-day tickets are usually available but prices rise.\n\n**Does the train go from Marrakech to Fes?** Not directly. You typically travel Marrakech → Casablanca → Fes with a change at Casablanca Voyageurs. The combined journey takes roughly 6–7 hours. Many travellers prefer a private car for this route — our [Marrakech to Fes guide](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/marrakech-to-fes-travel-guide) covers both options.\n\n**Is Al Boraq the same as TGV?** It uses Alstom technology derived from the TGV platform and is operated under a similar model, but it is a distinct Moroccan service run by ONCF.\n\n**How do I get from Casablanca airport to the train?** A direct airport shuttle train runs from Casablanca Mohammed V Airport to Casablanca Voyageurs station, taking roughly 30 minutes. From Casablanca Voyageurs you can connect to trains across Morocco. It is one of the most straightforward airport rail links in Africa.\n\n_Planning a multi-city Morocco itinerary?_ _Our [Marrakech tours](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/marrakech-tours), [Fes tours](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/fes-tours) and [imperial cities](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/imperial-cities) pages show how private guiding makes the most of each stop — arrivals handled, guides waiting, no logistics left to chance._\n\nTags\n\n[#Morocco trains](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog?search=Morocco%20trains) [#ONCF](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog?search=ONCF) [#Al Boraq](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog?search=Al%20Boraq) [#Morocco travel tips](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog?search=Morocco%20travel%20tips) [#train in Morocco](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog?search=train%20in%20Morocco) [#Morocco transport](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog?search=Morocco%20transport) [#Casablanca Marrakech train](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog?search=Casablanca%20Marrakech%20train)\n\nShare this article\n\nShare\n\nSM\n\nWritten by\n\n## Related Articles\nContinue your journey through Morocco with these curated reads\n\n[![Morocco Private Tour vs Group Tour: An Honest Comparison](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/_next/image?url=%2Fimages%2Fdestinations%2Fmarrakech-hero.webp&w=1920&q=75&dpl=dpl_49TJ23V5EjQgb9pMXRRkPmsnQUP7)\\\n\\\nTravel Planning\\\n\\\n10 min readOctober 23, 2026\\\n\\\n**Morocco Private Tour vs Group Tour: An Honest Comparison** \\\n\\\nGroup tours run $540–$2,700 per person and deliver Morocco's highlights efficiently. Private tours start higher but give you the itinerary, vehicle, guide, and accommodation entirely your own. Here is when each is the right choice — with real numbers and a verdict.\\\n\\\nRead Article](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/morocco-private-tour-vs-group-tour)[![Morocco Travel 2027: Season-by-Season Planning Guide](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/_next/image?url=%2Fimages%2Fgenerated%2Fgen-camel-caravan-sunset-dunes.webp&w=1920&q=75&dpl=dpl_49TJ23V5EjQgb9pMXRRkPmsnQUP7)\\\n\\\nTravel Planning\\\n\\\n13 min readOctober 23, 2026\\\n\\\n**Morocco Travel 2027: Season-by-Season Planning Guide** \\\n\\\nSpring 2027 (March–May) will be Morocco's peak season and should be booked by December–January. Autumn is the next-best window with more flexibility. The Sahara is finest from November through April. This guide covers 2027 trip planning month by month — with honest booking windows from an operator managing inventory now.\\\n\\\nRead Article](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/morocco-travel-2027)[![Morocco Private Tour for One Person: Real Solo Costs (2026)](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/_next/image?url=%2Fimages%2Fgenerated%2Fgen-couple-guide-riad-doorway-koutoubia.webp&w=1920&q=75&dpl=dpl_49TJ23V5EjQgb9pMXRRkPmsnQUP7)\\\n\\\nTravel Planning\\\n\\\n11 min readOctober 16, 2026\\\n\\\n**Morocco Private Tour for One Person: Real Solo Costs (2026)** \\\n\\\nWhat a private Morocco tour for one actually costs in 2026 — the real per-day math, why solo doesn't cost twice as much as traveling with a partner, and when going private solo beats joining a group.\\\n\\\nRead Article](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/morocco-solo-private-tour-cost)\n\n[View All Articles](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog)",

        "markdown": "Season MMXXVIFrom Marrakech to the Sahara, privately kept. [Plan Your Journey](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/inquiry)\n\n![Morocco Train Travel: The Complete Guide to ONCF Rail (2026)](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/_next/image?url=%2Fimages%2Fgenerated%2Fgen-hassan-ii-mosque-casablanca-sea.webp&w=1920&q=75&dpl=dpl_49TJ23V5EjQgb9pMXRRkPmsnQUP7)\n\n[Back to Journal](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog)\n\nTravel Planning\n\n# Morocco Train Travel: The Complete Guide to ONCF Rail (2026)\n\nOctober 23, 2026\n\n8 min read\n\nHow to travel Morocco by train: routes, tickets and tips for the Al Boraq high-speed service and Al Atlas express network — the most comfortable way to connect the imperial cities.\n\n1,549 words\n\n8 min read\n\nWritten and checked by the [Serenity Morocco Tours travel desk](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/about/team) — Travel designers, drivers and licensed guides\n\nLast reviewedOctober 23, 2026\n\nNewsletter\n\nGet Morocco Travel Insights\n\nSubscribe\n\nMorocco's rail network is one of Africa's most developed — clean, reasonably punctual and a genuine pleasure for journeys that matter: Casablanca to Rabat in under an hour, Rabat to Fes in around three hours, Casablanca to Marrakech in roughly three. Operated by ONCF (Office National des Chemins de Fer du Maroc), the network spans roughly 2,000 km and links Morocco's largest cities with a mix of high-speed and classic express services. The flagship Al Boraq TGV-style train slashed the journey from Tangier to Casablanca to around two hours; the Al Atlas expresses handle the inland routes. For travellers doing a multi-city Morocco itinerary — imperial cities, Atlantic coast, Sahara gateway towns — knowing how to use the trains well means faster connections, a lower environmental footprint and a more relaxed arrival than a long road transfer. This guide covers every main route, how to book, what to expect on board and when a private transfer still makes more sense.\n\n## [\\#](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/morocco-train-travel-guide\\#at-a-glance) At a Glance\n\n\\| \\| \\|\n\\|---\\|---\\|\n\\| **Operator** \\| ONCF (Office National des Chemins de Fer) \\|\n\\| **High-speed service** \\| Al Boraq: Tangier ↔ Casablanca, roughly 2h10m \\|\n\\| **Classic express** \\| Al Atlas: Casablanca ↔ Marrakech, Casablanca ↔ Fes, Fes ↔ Oujda \\|\n\\| **Booking** \\| oncf.ma website, ONCF app, or station counters \\|\n\\| **Book ahead** \\| Up to around 3 months in advance; earlier is usually cheaper \\|\n\\| **High-speed to Marrakech** \\| Under construction; tentative completion around 2029 \\|\n\\| **Airport connection** \\| Direct train, Casablanca airport ↔ Casablanca Voyageurs, roughly 30 minutes \\|\n\n## [\\#](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/morocco-train-travel-guide\\#moroccos-rail-hub-casablanca) Morocco's Rail Hub: Casablanca\n\nNearly every main rail journey in Morocco passes through or originates at Casablanca, and specifically **Casablanca Voyageurs** — the country's principal station and interchange. From here, trains reach Rabat in roughly 45 minutes, Kenitra in around an hour, Fes in approximately 3h15m–3h45m, and Marrakech in around 2h55m–3h15m depending on the service. Tangier is served by the Al Boraq high-speed line in roughly two hours via Kenitra and Rabat.\n\nFor visitors flying into Mohammed V Airport, there is a direct airport shuttle train to Casablanca Voyageurs (roughly 30 minutes), making it easy to continue by rail without entering the city at all. If you have time to stop, our [Casablanca travel guide](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/complete-casablanca-travel-guide-2026) covers the Hassan II Mosque, the Corniche and the city's underrated culinary scene.\n\n## [\\#](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/morocco-train-travel-guide\\#al-boraq-moroccos-high-speed-train) Al Boraq: Morocco's High-Speed Train\n\nThe **Al Boraq** is Morocco's flagship rail service and, at its 2018 launch, became Africa's first high-speed train. It connects **Tangier Ville** and **Casablanca Voyageurs** in roughly 2 hours 10 minutes — down from around 5 hours on the classic line. Along the way it stops at Kénitra, Rabat-Agdal and Casablanca Port. The rolling stock is modern and comfortable: fully air-conditioned, wide seats, at-seat power sockets and a quiet-car option in most formations.\n\nFares are somewhat higher than the Al Atlas expresses, but not dramatically. Second-class tickets on Al Boraq have been available from around 99–150 MAD for the Tangier–Casablanca journey (prices vary by booking date and class — check current fares on oncf.ma before assuming any figure holds). First class is typically a meaningful step up in comfort for a moderate additional cost. Book in advance when you have a fixed date: prices tend to rise closer to departure and the midday trains sell out.\n\nTangier is also a natural starting point for travellers arriving by ferry from southern Spain (Algeciras or Tarifa); the ferry port is a short taxi ride from Tangier Ville station. Our [Tangier food guide](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/tangier-food-guide) and [Chefchaouen day trip guide](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/chefchaouen-day-trip) cover what to do at either end of the line.\n\n## [\\#](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/morocco-train-travel-guide\\#al-atlas-express-the-inland-network) Al Atlas Express: The Inland Network\n\nFor routes the Al Boraq doesn't serve — and that is most of Morocco's interior — the **Al Atlas** classic express trains do the work. These are reliable long-distance services with air-conditioning, comfortable second and first-class carriages, and sometimes a dining car on the longer routes. Journey times are slower than the high-speed line, but fares are lower, and on scenic routes like the Casablanca–Fes leg the views through the Middle Atlas foothills are a reward in themselves.\n\nKey Al Atlas routes and approximate journey times (confirm before travel, as schedules change):\n\n\\| Route \\| Approximate time \\|\n\\|---\\|---\\|\n\\| Casablanca ↔ Marrakech \\| 2h55m – 3h15m \\|\n\\| Casablanca ↔ Fes \\| 3h15m – 3h45m (via Rabat, Meknes) \\|\n\\| Casablanca ↔ Meknes \\| 2h30m – 3h \\|\n\\| Casablanca ↔ Rabat \\| around 45 minutes \\|\n\\| Fes ↔ Oujda \\| around 3h30m – 4h \\|\n\nFor a [Fes itinerary](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/fes-2-day-itinerary), the Al Atlas train from Casablanca is typically the most comfortable way in. Meknes — one of Morocco's most overlooked imperial cities — sits just off the Casablanca–Fes line, making it easy to combine both on a single trip; our [Meknes day trip guide](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/meknes-day-trip-from-fes) explains how to fit it in without a detour.\n\n## [\\#](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/morocco-train-travel-guide\\#the-routes-that-still-require-road-travel) The Routes That Still Require Road Travel\n\nA few popular Morocco itinerary legs are not yet served by rail, and a private transfer or bus is the practical option:\n\n- **Marrakech to Essaouira:** no rail link — roughly 2h30m–3h by private car\n- **Marrakech to Agadir:** no rail link — around 3–4 hours by road\n- **Fes to Chefchaouen:** no rail — best by private car or CTM bus (around 2h30m–3h)\n- **Marrakech to the Sahara (Merzouga or Zagora):** no train to the desert; private car over one or two days is the standard choice\n\nA high-speed extension connecting Kenitra and Marrakech is reportedly under construction, with an estimated completion around 2029 — though large infrastructure projects often follow their own timelines. Until then, the Al Atlas Casablanca–Marrakech service is the only rail option for the Red City. For road-transfer logistics, our [Marrakech to Fes travel guide](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/marrakech-to-fes-travel-guide) weighs all the options, and [private driver versus self-drive](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/morocco-private-driver-vs-self-drive) is useful reading before you decide.\n\n## [\\#](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/morocco-train-travel-guide\\#booking-and-practical-tips) Booking and Practical Tips\n\n**Where to book:** Tickets are available on the **oncf.ma** website, the ONCF mobile app, or at any station counter. Online booking accepts major credit and debit cards; the site is primarily in French and Arabic. Tickets typically go on sale around three months ahead; early purchase gives you lower fares and more seat choice.\n\n**At the station:**\n\n- Arrive 15–20 minutes early at busy hubs like Casablanca Voyageurs or Marrakech\n- Some stations have ticket barriers that require composting (stamping) your ticket before the platform — look for the small yellow machines near the gates\n- Large bags go in the racks at the end of each carriage; overhead bins are for carry-on size only\n\n**Class choice:** Second class is comfortable and fine for most journeys. First class is noticeably quieter on the longer Al Atlas runs and worth the modest premium on a 3-hour journey.\n\n**Children and discounts:** ONCF typically offers reduced fares for young children and occasional promotional passes (such as the SAYFI summer pass); confirm current terms and validity directly on oncf.ma as promotions change frequently.\n\nFor travellers who prefer a seamless arrival — a private transfer from the station, a guide ready at the riad — our [Marrakech tours](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/marrakech-tours), [Fes tours](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/fes-tours) and [Casablanca tours](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/casablanca-tours) teams handle the logistics end-to-end. The [imperial cities circuit](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/imperial-cities) is a natural fit for combining the rail network with private guiding.\n\n## [\\#](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/morocco-train-travel-guide\\#faq) FAQ\n\n**Is train travel in Morocco comfortable?**\nBroadly yes, for city-to-city journeys. The Al Boraq high-speed trains are modern and well-maintained. Al Atlas expresses are older but clean, air-conditioned and reliable. The main limitation is that many popular destinations — the Sahara, Chefchaouen, Essaouira — are not on the network.\n\n**How much does a Morocco train ticket cost?**\nPrices vary by route, service and booking date. Al Boraq second-class tickets have started from around 99–150 MAD for Tangier–Casablanca; Al Atlas second-class fares for Casablanca–Marrakech or Casablanca–Fes are typically lower. Check oncf.ma for current fares — early booking is cheaper.\n\n**Do I need to book Morocco train tickets in advance?**\nIt's advisable, especially in July and August, at Eid holidays and on Al Boraq. Tickets go on sale around three months ahead on oncf.ma and at station counters. Same-day tickets are usually available but prices rise.\n\n**Does the train go from Marrakech to Fes?**\nNot directly. You typically travel Marrakech → Casablanca → Fes with a change at Casablanca Voyageurs. The combined journey takes roughly 6–7 hours. Many travellers prefer a private car for this route — our [Marrakech to Fes guide](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog/marrakech-to-fes-travel-guide) covers both options.\n\n**Is Al Boraq the same as TGV?**\nIt uses Alstom technology derived from the TGV platform and is operated under a similar model, but it is a distinct Moroccan service run by ONCF. At launch it was widely described as Africa's first high-speed rail line, with top speeds of around 320 km/h on the fastest sections.\n\n**How do I get from Casablanca airport to the train?**\nA direct airport shuttle train runs from Casablanca Mohammed V Airport to Casablanca Voyageurs station, taking roughly 30 minutes. From Casablanca Voyageurs you can connect to trains across Morocco. It is one of the most straightforward airport rail links in Africa.\n\n* * *\n\n_Planning a multi-city Morocco itinerary? Our [Marrakech tours](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/marrakech-tours), [Fes tours](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/fes-tours) and [imperial cities](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/imperial-cities) pages show how private guiding makes the most of each stop — arrivals handled, guides waiting, no logistics left to chance._\n\nTags\n\n[#Morocco trains](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog?search=Morocco%20trains) [#ONCF](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog?search=ONCF) [#Al Boraq](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog?search=Al%20Boraq) [#Morocco travel tips](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog?search=Morocco%20travel%20tips) [#train in Morocco](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog?search=train%20in%20Morocco) [#Morocco transport](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog?search=Morocco%20transport) [#Casablanca Marrakech train](https://www.serenitymoroccotours.com/blog?search=Casablanca%20Marrakech%20train)\n\nShare this article\n\nShare\n\nSM\n\nWritten by\n\n### Serenity Morocco editorial team\n\nSerenity Morocco Tours\n\nOur in-house editors and Morocco travel designers research, write, and fact-check every guide — drawing on first-hand local knowledge across the country's cities, desert, mountains, and coast.\n\n[LinkedIn](https://www.linkedin.com/in/serenity-morocco-tours-a551a3419)\n\nYour Journey, Tailor-Made\n\n## Ready to experience Travel Planning for yourself?\n\nSkip the guesswork.
